| **`RESEARCH FINDING`** | **The available source indicates that **s.26** provides for non-liability for a co-trustee's defaults, but that **in the absence of an express declaration to the contrary in the instrument of trust** a trustee **is** liable where he has delivered trust property to a co-trustee without seeing to its proper application; **where he allows a co-trustee to receive trust property and fails to make due enquiry into the co-trustee's dealings with it, or allows him to retain it longer than the circumstances of the case reasonably require; **and in the further cases the section lists. **Section 27** provides that **where co-trustees jointly commit a breach of trust, or where one of them by his neglect enables the other to commit a breach, each is liable to the beneficiary for the whole of the loss** occasioned by that breach. **Contribution between co-trustees is available: **as between themselves, one who is less guilty and has had to refund the loss may compel the more guilty to make it good to the extent of the assets received, and if all are equally guilty, any one or more who has refunded may compel the others to contribute. **On indemnity, the source records that a trustee is entitled to recover from the beneficiary personally, on whose behalf he acted and at whose request, express or implied, he made the payment, the amount of such expenses — **but that nothing in the section entitles a trustee to be indemnified who has, in committing the breach of trust, been guilty of fraud**** |
| **`PRIMARY SOURCE`** | ### **`SRC-37` — India Code, Indian Trusts Act, 1882, ss.26, 27 and the indemnity provision. **`TIER 1`**. **Corroborated by **`SRC-38`**** |
| **`SOURCE DATE / EFFECTIVE DATE`** | **`CURRENT`** |
| **`RELEVANT CONDITION`** | **Co-trusteeship. **An express declaration in the instrument of trust can displace s.26's default. **Fraud defeats indemnity** |
| **`ENTITY DEPENDENCY`** | **`YES` — trust form only** |
| **`FACT DEPENDENCY`** | **`YES`** |
| **`INTERPRETATION RISK`** | **`LOW` as to the text** |
| **`WHAT THE SOURCE ACTUALLY ESTABLISHES`** | **That co-trusteeship carries **exposure for the whole loss** where the breach is joint or where neglect enables it; **that **passive trusteeship** — delivering property without seeing to its application, or failing to make due enquiry — is itself a route to liability; **and that indemnity is available but is defeated by fraud** |
| **`WHAT THE SOURCE DOES NOT ESTABLISH`** | **That any of the four unpaid control roles would be trustees. **That the architecture's separation-of-duties design would satisfy the *"due enquiry"* standard — **which is a question for a professional, and is the single most useful question this finding raises** |
| **`SEWAK SATHI CONSEQUENCE`** | **`CLARIFICATION REQUIRED`. **The architecture's four-hand rule and its segregation of duties — **[appendix.txt:6925–6958]**, **CEA §XX.1** — exist to prevent one person controlling a payment. **Under s.26, **failure to enquire into what a co-holder does is itself a ground of liability**, which means the architecture's controls are not merely prudent but potentially protective, **and their absence is potentially aggravating**. **This is a rare case in this research where an architectural design feature has a direct liability consequence** |
| **`PROFESSIONAL VALIDATION REQUIRED`** | **`YES`** |
| **`CLASSIFICATION`** | ### **`SUPPORTED BY PRIMARY SOURCE`** |

### `RF-20` — a registered society: legal personality, vesting of property, and who is sued

| Field | **Content** |
|---|---|
| **`RESEARCH ID`** | **`RF-20`** |
| **`ARCHITECTURAL ID`** | **`PVR-22`, `SR-01`; **`LD-02`, `LD-08`, `LD-21`; **`OPT-B`; **`FC-01 … FC-04`; **`CQ-02`, `CQ-34`; **`GC-01` node 2** |
| **`QUESTION`** | **Is a society registered under the Societies Registration Act, 1860 a legal person; **in whom does its property vest; **and against whom are proceedings brought?** |
| **`LEGAL / TAX DOMAIN`** | **Company and entity law; **liability** |
| **`RESEARCH FINDING`** | **The sources conflict, and the conflict is recorded rather than resolved. **The Act's own provisions, as reproduced, are: **s.1** — at least **seven** members are required to constitute a society; **s.5** — *"the property, movable and immovable belonging to a society registered under this Act, if not vested in trustees, shall be deemed to be vested, for the time being, in the governing body of such society"*, and in all proceedings civil and criminal may be described as the property of the governing body; **s.6** — every registered society may sue or be sued **in the name of the president, chairman, principal secretary or trustees**, as determined by its rules and regulations, or in the name of a person appointed by the governing body; **s.8** — enforcement of a judgment is against the society's property; **s.10** — a member in arrears of subscription, or who has or detains society property contrary to the regulations, or who injures or destroys society property, **may be sued**; **s.16** — the governing body means the governors, council, directors, committee, trustees or other body to which the management of the society's affairs is entrusted. **Against that text, one source reports a **Constitution Bench of the Supreme Court**, described as the *Unani Tibia College* case, holding that **a registered society is not a corporation**; **that the Act confers privileges, some of considerable importance and some analogous to those of a corporation, but that *"there is really no incorporation in the sense in which that word is legally understood"*; **that the society, being unincorporated, **cannot sue or be sued in its own name**, though it is given the statutory privilege of suing and being sued in the name of its trustees; **that the phrase *"property belonging to a society"* in s.5 **does not give the society a corporate status** in the matter of holding or acquiring property but merely describes property vested in the trustees or governing body for the time being; **and that a society, registered or unregistered, **may not be prosecuted in criminal court**, is not capable of ownership of property, and cannot sue or be sued in its own name. **Other secondary sources state the opposite: **that societies *"acquire perpetual succession and the capacity to acquire, hold and dispose of property, to contract, and to sue or be sued"*; **that *"a registered society gains the status of a legal entity"*; **and that societies *"gain juridical personality upon registration"*. **The same sources also record that enforcement of judgments targets society property, shielding named officers from personal liability on application to the executing court under s.8, and that members' liability is confined to unpaid subscriptions or wilful damage** |
| **`PRIMARY SOURCE`** | ### **`SRC-72` — reports the Constitution Bench holding and quotes it at length. **`TIER 2/3`; **the judgment itself was **not obtained in this research**. **`SRC-73`** reproduces ss.5, 6, 10 and 16. **`SRC-74`, `SRC-75`, `SRC-76`** assert juristic personality. **The Societies Registration Act, 1860 was **not obtained from India Code in this research**** |
| **`SOURCE DATE / EFFECTIVE DATE`** | **Act of 1860, as amended state by state. **The reported judgment is undated in the source located. **`CURRENT APPLICABILITY NOT ESTABLISHED` as to the judgment's citation, its date, and whether it remains good law** |
| **`RELEVANT CONDITION`** | **State amendments vary materially and society law is administered state by state — **`FC-E6`**, **`CQ-34`**. **The RBI's KYC Directions treat societies as *"juridical persons"* for account-opening purposes and require documents establishing *"the legal existence of such an entity/juridical person"* — **`RF-33`** — which is a regulatory usage and does not resolve the private-law question** |
| **`ENTITY DEPENDENCY`** | ### **`YES — this is the entity question itself`** |
| **`FACT DEPENDENCY`** | **`NO`** |
| **`INTERPRETATION RISK`** | ### **`HIGH — a Supreme Court Constitution Bench and several secondary sources are in direct opposition, and the judgment was not obtained`** |
| **`WHAT THE SOURCE ACTUALLY ESTABLISHES`** | **That the Act vests property in the **governing body** rather than in an abstract society; **that suits are brought in the name of a **named officer or trustee**; **that judgment is enforced against society property; **that members are personally suable in defined circumstances; **and that a **real and documented conflict** exists about whether a society is a legal person** |
| **`WHAT THE SOURCE DOES NOT ESTABLISH`** | **Which side of the conflict is right. **This instrument does not resolve it and must not. **It does not establish the position in any particular state, and it does not establish whether the two positions can be reconciled as a distinction between *juristic personality for procedural purposes* and *incorporation in the strict sense*** |
| **`SEWAK SATHI CONSEQUENCE`** | ### **`BLOCKED PENDING PROFESSIONAL VALIDATION`, recorded as **`SCF-01`**. **This is the most consequential conflict found in this research, because **`OPT-B`** is one of the candidates the corpus names most often and **`ID-1`'s `FC-01`** records legal personality as the first criterion of all. **A candidate whose legal personality is contested at Constitution Bench level cannot be scored by an instrument. **It also bears on **`PVR-22`**: **if suits run in the name of named officers, then the individuals holding office are the persons named in proceedings even where enforcement reaches only society property** |
| **`PROFESSIONAL VALIDATION REQUIRED`** | **`YES`** |
| **`CLASSIFICATION`** | ### **`SOURCE CONFLICT`** |

### `RF-21` — fraudulent conduct of business: unlimited personal liability, and its two strict conditions

| Field | **Content** |
|---|---|
| **`RESEARCH ID`** | **`RF-21`** |
| **`ARCHITECTURAL ID`** | **`PVR-22`; **`LD-03`, `LD-04`, `LD-11`; **`RSK-1`, `RSK-2`, `RSK-9`; **`OPT-D`, `OPT-A`; **`CQ-12`, `CQ-22`; **`G-01 … G-16`** |
| **`QUESTION`** | **When may directors, managers or officers be made personally liable for a company's debts?** |
| **`LEGAL / TAX DOMAIN`** | **Company law; **liability** |
| **`RESEARCH FINDING`** | **The available sources indicate that **s.339(1) of the Companies Act, 2013** provides that **if, in the course of the winding up of a company**, it appears that any business of the company has been carried on **with intent to defraud creditors of the company or any other persons, or for any fraudulent purpose**, the Tribunal — on the application of the Official Liquidator, the Company Liquidator, **or any creditor or contributory of the company** — may declare that any person who is or has been a **director, manager or officer** of the company, **or any person who was knowingly a party** to the carrying on of the business in that manner, **shall be personally responsible, without any limitation of liability, for all or any of the debts or other liabilities of the company** as the Tribunal may direct. **The Tribunal may also make provision for making that liability a charge on any debt or obligation due from the company to him, or on any mortgage or charge on, or interest in, company assets held by or vested in him or in any person on his behalf. **Commentary records that the provision corresponds to ss.542 and 543 of the Companies Act, 1956; **that liability under it is civil but may trigger prosecution under **s.447**, punishment for fraud; **that the class of persons caught is **not restricted to insiders** such as employees, directors or partners, and is wide enough to include fraud on behalf of third parties such as other corporate persons and creditors; **and that **s.340** separately empowers the Tribunal to enquire into and order repayment or contribution to the assets by any **promoter, director, manager, company liquidator or officer** who has misapplied, retained, or become liable or accountable for, any money or property of the company, or who has been guilty of **misfeasance or breach of trust**, with **s.341** extending the liability under ss.339 and 340. **A National Company Law Tribunal order located in this research recites s.339 in these terms and records an application seeking prosecution under both ss.339 and 340, including against a person for holding himself out as a director and purporting to act as such after resignation** |
| **`PRIMARY SOURCE`** | ### **`SRC-40` — National Company Law Tribunal, Mumbai Bench-I, CP No. 100 of 2019, quoting ss.339 and 340. **`TIER 1 — OFFICIAL JUDICIAL SOURCE`**. **Corroborated by **`SRC-41`** (full text of s.339(1)), **`SRC-42`**** |
| **`SOURCE DATE / EFFECTIVE DATE`** | **Companies Act, 2013; **the NCLT order is dated 2024. **`CURRENT`** |
| **`RELEVANT CONDITION`** | ### **`TWO, BOTH STRICT: (1) the company must be **in the course of winding up**; **and (2) the business must have been carried on **with intent to defraud** creditors or any other persons, **or for any fraudulent purpose**, or the person must have been **knowingly a party** to it. **Absent both, the section does not operate** |
| **`ENTITY DEPENDENCY`** | **`YES` — company form only, and only in winding up** |
| **`FACT DEPENDENCY`** | **`YES` — on intent, which is a question of fact** |
| **`INTERPRETATION RISK`** | **`LOW` as to the text and the conditions** |
| **`WHAT THE SOURCE ACTUALLY ESTABLISHES`** | **That personal liability without limit exists for directors, managers, officers **and knowing third parties**, but only on winding up and only on fraud; **and that a separate misfeasance route exists at s.340 for misapplication, retention, misfeasance or breach of trust, carrying criminal liability** |
| **`WHAT THE SOURCE DOES NOT ESTABLISH`** | **That a director is personally liable for a company's **ordinary** debts. **The opposite is the general rule of limited liability, and nothing located here displaces it. **It does not establish that anyone here would be a director, or that a Section 8 company's officers are outside the section** |
| **`SEWAK SATHI CONSEQUENCE`** | **`CLARIFICATION REQUIRED`, and it **narrows** an exposure the architecture had left open. **`ID-1`'s **`LD-03`** and **`RSK-1`** asked about personal exposure for corporate debts without a source. **This research supplies the source and its two strict conditions, and the answer is that the exposure is **narrow and fault-based**, not general. **That is a material narrowing — but it narrows a question that arises only if a company is formed, and **no conclusion about the four unpaid control roles follows from it** |
| **`PROFESSIONAL VALIDATION REQUIRED`** | **`LATER`** |
| **`CLASSIFICATION`** | ### **`SUPPORTED BY PRIMARY SOURCE`** |

### `RF-22` — directors' liability for a private company's unrecoverable tax dues

| Field | **Content** |
|---|---|
| **`RESEARCH ID`** | **`RF-22`** |
| **`ARCHITECTURAL ID`** | **`PVR-22`; **`LD-04`, `LD-12`; **`RSK-3`, `RSK-9`; **`TX-01`, `TX-13`; **`OPT-D`; **`CQ-12`, `CQ-17`, `CQ-22`; **`SEP-1`, `SEP-2`** |
| **`QUESTION`** | **May directors be made personally liable for a company's tax that cannot be recovered from the company?** |
| **`LEGAL / TAX DOMAIN`** | **Tax; **company law; **liability** |
| **`RESEARCH FINDING`** | **The available sources indicate that **s.179(1) of the Income-tax Act, 1961** provided that where tax due from a **private company** in respect of any income of any previous year **cannot be recovered**, every person who was a **director of that private company at any time during the relevant previous year** is **jointly and severally liable** for the payment of such tax, **unless he proves that the non-recovery cannot be attributed to any gross neglect, misfeasance or breach of duty on his part** in relation to the affairs of the company. **The conditions were treated as jurisdictional: **the Revenue must first establish that the tax cannot be recovered from the company, and a show-cause notice that is silent on the steps taken against the company has been held unsustainable. **The provision reaches **tax** as defined by the Act and, on the authority located, **not penalty and not interest**. **Sub-section (2) addresses a private company later converted into a public company, and excludes liability for tax of any assessment year before 1962-63. **The Central Board of Direct Taxes explained the provision in **Circular No. 179 dated 30 September 1975**, recording that the amending legislation extended personal liability to cases where the company had **not** gone into liquidation. **Reported decisions include one in which a director discharged the burden by showing lack of financial control, lack of decision-making power and a very limited role, with the court recording that the legislature used the words *"gross neglect"* and not mere neglect, and that gross negligence is to be viewed in the context of the non-recovery of the company's tax dues rather than the general functioning of the company. **Commentary records that minority, independent and nominee directors face a real difficulty in demonstrating limited control, and that **directors and officers insurance** may be considered in response. **As to the current statute: **one source states that the 1961 Act was repealed and replaced by the Income-tax Act, 2025 with effect from 1 April 2026, that **s.179 of the 2025 Act is now a general anti-avoidance provision** dealing with impermissible avoidance arrangements and no longer addresses director liability, and that director liability for unpaid company tax is instead addressed **in substantially the same terms in s.323 of the 2025 Act**** |
| **`PRIMARY SOURCE`** | **`SRC-43`**, **`SRC-44`** — **Tier 2**, on s.179(1), the jurisdictional conditions, the CBDT circular and the reported decisions. **`SRC-45`** — **Tier 3**, for the mapping of s.179 to s.323 of the 2025 Act. **The text of neither Act was obtained in this research** |
| **`SOURCE DATE / EFFECTIVE DATE`** | **s.179 of the 1961 Act: **in force until 31 March 2026**. **The 2025 Act: **from 1 April 2026** — **`DTC-01`**. **The section number in the current statute is **`CURRENT-LAW PROFESSIONAL VERIFICATION REQUIRED`**, because the mapping rests on a single Tier 3 source and **other section mappings located in this research conflict with each other — see **`SCF-02`**** |
| **`RELEVANT CONDITION`** | ### **`FOUR: (1) a **private company** — the provision as located does not extend to a public company save via s.179(2); **(2) tax **due**; **(3) tax that **cannot be recovered** from the company, which the Revenue must establish first; **and (4) directorship **at any time during the relevant previous year**. **The escape is a **proof** burden on the director** |
| **`ENTITY DEPENDENCY`** | **`YES` — private company form specifically** |
| **`FACT DEPENDENCY`** | **`YES` — on tax being due and unrecoverable, and on the director's actual role** |
| **`INTERPRETATION RISK`** | **`MEDIUM` as to the 1961 provision, which is well documented. **`HIGH` as to its current section number** |
| **`WHAT THE SOURCE ACTUALLY ESTABLISHES`** | **That Indian tax law contains, and on the available source continues to contain in substantially the same terms, a **reverse-onus personal liability** for directors of a private company where the company's tax cannot be recovered; **that the liability is joint and several; **that it reaches tax but not penalty or interest; **and that the defence is a proof burden discharged by evidence of limited control** |
| **`WHAT THE SOURCE DOES NOT ESTABLISH`** | **The current section number. **That any tax would be due here — **no entity, no income, no registration**. **And whether a **Section 8 company** is a *"private company"* for this purpose, which turns on how it is registered — a question this research does not answer and **must not assume**** |
| **`SEWAK SATHI CONSEQUENCE`** | ### **`BLOCKED PENDING PROFESSIONAL VALIDATION`. **`ID-1`'s **`LD-12`** and **`RSK-3`** asked about personal exposure for organisational tax. **This research establishes that such a regime exists and identifies its four conditions and its reverse onus — **and establishes that the answer for a Section 8 company depends on a registration fact nobody has. **It also identifies two mitigations recorded in the sources: **documentary evidence of limited control**, such as board resolutions and financial records, and **directors and officers insurance**. **Neither is a recommendation** |
| **`PROFESSIONAL VALIDATION REQUIRED`** | **`YES`** |
| **`CLASSIFICATION`** | ### **`PARTIALLY SUPPORTED` — the substance by Tier 2; **the current section number by Tier 3 alone and in conflict with other mappings** |

### `RF-23` — limited liability partnership: the shield, and the two routes through it

| Field | **Content** |
|---|---|
| **`RESEARCH ID`** | **`RF-23`** |
| **`ARCHITECTURAL ID`** | **`PVR-22`, `SR-01`; **`LD-03`; **`OPT-E`; **`FC-E1`, `FC-E2`; **`CQ-04`, `CQ-22`** |
| **`QUESTION`** | **To what extent is a partner of a limited liability partnership personally liable?** |
| **`LEGAL / TAX DOMAIN`** | **LLP law; **liability** |
| **`RESEARCH FINDING`** | **The available sources indicate that an LLP is a **body corporate and a legal entity separate from its partners**, with perpetual succession, capable in its own name of acquiring, owning, holding and disposing of property, movable and immovable, tangible and intangible, and of suing and being sued. **The liabilities of an LLP are met out of the **properties of the LLP**. **Section 28(1)** provides that a partner is **not personally liable, directly or indirectly, for an obligation of the LLP solely by reason of being a partner**. **Section 28(2)** provides that ss.27(3) and 28(1) **do not affect the personal liability of a partner for his own wrongful act or omission**, but that a partner is **not personally liable for the wrongful act or omission of any other partner**. **Section 27** makes the LLP liable for a partner's wrongful act or omission done in the course of the LLP's business or with its authority, and provides that the LLP is **not bound** by an act of a partner dealing with a person where the partner had no authority and that person knew it, or did not know or believe him to be a partner. **The shield is withdrawn where the LLP or any of its partners carries on business **with intent to defraud creditors of the LLP or any other person, or for any fraudulent purpose**: **the liability of the LLP **and of the partners concerned is then unlimited**, though the LLP is not liable if it establishes that the act was without its knowledge or authority; **and every person who was knowingly a party to the carrying on of business with such intent is punishable with imprisonment and fine. **Every partner is an **agent of the LLP but not of other partners**. **A person who represents himself, or knowingly permits himself to be represented, as a partner is liable to any person who on that basis gives credit to the LLP, and the LLP is liable to the extent of the credit received or any financial benefit derived from it. **An LLP must have **at least two individuals as designated partners, at least one of whom must be resident in India** — defined by reference to a stay of not less than **182 days** in the immediately preceding year — and **if there is no designated partner, or only one at any time, each partner is deemed to be a designated partner**. **Designated partners are responsible for doing all acts, matters and things required for compliance with the Act and are **liable to all penalties imposed on the LLP** for contravention. **For taxation an LLP is treated **on par with a partnership firm** under the Partnership Act, 1932. **There is no limit on the maximum number of partners, and an individual or a body corporate may be a partner** |
| **`PRIMARY SOURCE`** | **`SRC-50`**, **`SRC-51`** — **Tier 2**, quoting ss.27, 28(1), 28(2) and 30 and the designated-partner provisions. **`SRC-52`** — **Tier 3**. **The Limited Liability Partnership Act, 2008 was **not obtained from India Code in this research**** |
| **`SOURCE DATE / EFFECTIVE DATE`** | **Act of 2008. **`CURRENT`. **One source records penalty figures for fraud of up to ₹5,00,000 and imprisonment up to two years under s.30, and annual-return filing within sixty days under s.35 with daily default fees — all **`VERIFIED — SECONDARY`**** |
| **`RELEVANT CONDITION`** | **The shield operates **solely by reason of being a partner**; **it never covers a partner's **own** wrongful act or omission; **and it is withdrawn entirely on fraud** |
| **`ENTITY DEPENDENCY`** | **`YES` — LLP form only. **`ID-1` records **`OPT-E`** as `NOT PRESENT IN CORPUS`, and **Rule G-19** forbids scoring it from corpus statements. **This research supplies statutory material for that candidate for the first time, and it is labelled **`PROFESSIONAL INPUT — NOT CURRENT ARCHITECTURE DECISION`**** |
| **`FACT DEPENDENCY`** | **`YES` — on whose act, and on fraud** |
| **`INTERPRETATION RISK`** | **`LOW` as to the structure. **`MEDIUM` as to whether the *deemed designated partner* rule would catch all four unpaid control roles** |
| **`WHAT THE SOURCE ACTUALLY ESTABLISHES`** | **That the LLP form offers a **statutory shield with two identified routes through it** — own wrongdoing and fraud — and that **designated partners carry penalty liability for the LLP's contraventions**, with a deeming rule that can catch every partner where the office is vacant** |
| **`WHAT THE SOURCE DOES NOT ESTABLISH`** | **That an LLP is available, appropriate or permitted for any of the four pillars. **It does not address charitable status, donations, or the eight-priority waterfall — and **`GC-01`** is untouched by this finding** |
| **`SEWAK SATHI CONSEQUENCE`** | **`CLARIFICATION REQUIRED`. **The deeming rule is the most architecturally significant element: **an LLP with no designated partner, or only one, makes **every** partner a designated partner and therefore liable to all penalties imposed on the LLP. **For an architecture whose **`APPROVES`** function is unassigned in thirteen of sixteen acts and whose vacancies are unfilled — **CEA Part XX** — a vacancy-triggered deeming rule is a direct and material risk** |
| **`PROFESSIONAL VALIDATION REQUIRED`** | **`YES`** |
| **`CLASSIFICATION`** | ### **`SUPPORTED BY SECONDARY SOURCE ONLY`** |

### `RF-24` — partnership: mutual agency, and joint and several liability without limit

| Field | **Content** |
|---|---|
| **`RESEARCH ID`** | **`RF-24`** |
| **`ARCHITECTURAL ID`** | **`PVR-22`, `SR-01`; **`LD-03`, `LD-22`; **`OPT-F`; **`RSK-1`; **`CQ-04`, `CQ-22`; **`SEP-1`, `SEP-2`** |
| **`QUESTION`** | **To what extent is a partner in an ordinary partnership personally liable?** |
| **`LEGAL / TAX DOMAIN`** | **Partnership law; **liability** |
| **`RESEARCH FINDING`** | **The available sources reproduce **s.18 of the Indian Partnership Act, 1932**: *"Subject to the provisions of this Act, a partner is the agent of the firm for the purposes of the business of the firm."* **Section 19(1)** confers **implied authority**: subject to s.22, the act of a partner done to carry on, in the usual way, business of the kind carried on by the firm **binds the firm**; **sub-section (2)** limits that implied authority in the absence of contrary usage or custom of trade. **Section 25** provides: *"Every partner is liable, jointly with all the other partners and also severally, for all acts of the firm done while he is a partner."* **The commentary located records that in India the liability of partners **for contracts as well as for torts** is joint and several — differing from England, where liability is joint for contracts and joint and several for torts; **that the liability is **unlimited**; **that a third party may sue any one partner severally or any two or more jointly, and no partner may plead that others should be joined; **that a partner who pays more than his share may claim **contribution** from his co-partners; **that a partner is **not** liable for acts of the firm done **before** he joined or **after** he ceased to be a partner; **and that no partner may rely on an internal agreement limiting his liability as against a third party. **The Act also defines an *act of the firm* as any act or omission by all the partners, or by any partner or agent of the firm, which gives rise to a right enforceable by or against the firm, and provides that the firm shall indemnify a partner in respect of payments made and liabilities incurred by him in the ordinary and proper conduct of the business of the firm, and in doing such an act in an emergency for the purpose of protecting the firm from loss as would be done by a person of ordinary prudence in his own case under similar circumstances** |
| **`PRIMARY SOURCE`** | **`SRC-53`**, **`SRC-54`** — reproductions of ss.18, 19, 25 and the surrounding chapter. **`TIER 3 HOSTS, TIER 1 CONTENT`. **The Act was not obtained from India Code** |
| **`SOURCE DATE / EFFECTIVE DATE`** | **Act No. IX of 1932, 8 April 1932. **`CURRENT`** |
| **`RELEVANT CONDITION`** | **A **partnership** must exist. **The act must be an **act of the firm**, done **while he is a partner**, and within the purposes of the firm's business** |
| **`ENTITY DEPENDENCY`** | **`YES` — partnership form only. **`OPT-F` is `NOT PRESENT IN CORPUS`, and this material is labelled **`PROFESSIONAL INPUT — NOT CURRENT ARCHITECTURE DECISION`**** |
| **`FACT DEPENDENCY`** | **`YES`** |
| **`INTERPRETATION RISK`** | **`LOW` as to the text** |
| **`WHAT THE SOURCE ACTUALLY ESTABLISHES`** | **That the partnership form carries **unlimited joint and several personal liability for every act of the firm**, in contract and in tort, with no shield other than the temporal limits of the partnership and a right of contribution between partners — together with a firm indemnity for acts done in the ordinary and proper conduct of the business and in a genuine emergency** |
| **`WHAT THE SOURCE DOES NOT ESTABLISH`** | **That a partnership exists or is contemplated. **`ID-1` **`FA-4`** and **`DIS-29`** record that no constitutive document of any kind exists. **It does not establish that a partnership would be available for a charitable object** |
| **`SEWAK SATHI CONSEQUENCE`** | **`CLARIFICATION REQUIRED`. **This is the **strongest personal-liability regime located in this research**, and the sharpest available contrast to **`RF-23`** and **`RF-21`**. **It is recorded because **`OPT-F`** is one of the ten candidates counsel must be given unfiltered, and because a comparison omitting the liability consequence of the partnership form would be incomplete. **No candidate is preferred by recording it. **The firm-indemnity limb is also noted, because it is the only indemnity provision located anywhere in this research that operates without an entity's own instrument** |
| **`PROFESSIONAL VALIDATION REQUIRED`** | **`LATER`** |
| **`CLASSIFICATION`** | ### **`SUPPORTED BY SECONDARY SOURCE ONLY`** |

### `RF-25` — the Good Samaritan protection, and the narrowness of its scope

| Field | **Content** |
|---|---|
| **`RESEARCH ID`** | **`RF-25`** |
| **`ARCHITECTURAL ID`** | **`PVR-22`; **`LD-14 … LD-17`; **`RSK-4`, `RSK-5`, `RSK-8`; **`L1`, `L4`, `L5`, `L8`; **`CQ-24`, `CQ-25`; **the protection doctrine; **`VG-1`, `VG-2`** |
| **`QUESTION`** | **Does Indian law protect a person who renders emergency assistance, and how far does that protection extend?** |
| **`LEGAL / TAX DOMAIN`** | **Liability; **safeguarding** |
| **`RESEARCH FINDING`** | **The available sources reproduce **s.134A of the Motor Vehicles Act, 1988**, inserted by the Motor Vehicles (Amendment) Act, 2019, headed *"Protection of Good Samaritans"*: **sub-section (1)** — *"A Good Samaritan shall not be liable for any civil or criminal action for any injury to or death of the victim of an accident **involving a motor vehicle**, where such injury or death resulted from the Good Samaritan's negligence in acting or failing to act while rendering emergency medical or non-medical care or assistance"*; **sub-section (2)** — the Central Government may by rules provide for the procedure for questioning or examination of the Good Samaritan, for disclosure of his personal information, and for such other related matters; **and the Explanation — *"Good Samaritan"* means a person who, **in good faith, voluntarily and without expectation of any reward or compensation**, renders emergency medical or non-medical care or assistance **at the scene of an accident** to the victim, or transports the victim to hospital. **The legislative history recorded is that the Supreme Court of India, on a petition brought by the SaveLIFE Foundation, pronounced judgment on **30 March 2016** instituting the country's first national, legally binding Good Samaritan protection guidelines, and that the 2019 amendment codified the protection into the statute. **Related material records that following those directions and the enactment, police may not compel a helper to stay or to reveal his identity, that a helper who chooses to be a witness may give his statement at a time and place of his convenience, and that the national emergency number is 112** |
| **`PRIMARY SOURCE`** | **`SRC-106`** — reproduces the section in English and in Hindi. **`SRC-103`, `SRC-104`, `SRC-105`, `SRC-107`** — corroborating. **`TIER 3 HOSTS, TIER 1 CONTENT`. **The Motor Vehicles Act text was not obtained from India Code, and the **Supreme Court judgment was not obtained**** |
| **`SOURCE DATE / EFFECTIVE DATE`** | **Inserted by the Motor Vehicles (Amendment) Act, **2019**; **the judgment is dated **30 March 2016**. **`CURRENT`** |
| **`RELEVANT CONDITION`** | ### **`THREE, AND THEY ARE NARROW: (1) the injury or death must be that of the victim of an accident **involving a motor vehicle**; **(2) the assistance must be rendered **in good faith, voluntarily and without expectation of any reward or compensation**; **and (3) the protection covers injury or death **resulting from the Good Samaritan's negligence** in acting or failing to act while rendering that care** |
| **`ENTITY DEPENDENCY`** | ### **`NO — the protection attaches to a person, and needs no entity`** |
| **`FACT DEPENDENCY`** | **`YES` — on the nature of the incident and on good faith** |
| **`INTERPRETATION RISK`** | ### **`HIGH` as to scope. **The section is confined by its own words to accidents involving a motor vehicle. **Whether any analogous protection exists for a non-road emergency — which is most of what the architecture's protection pillar contemplates — was **not established by any source located in this research**** |
| **`WHAT THE SOURCE ACTUALLY ESTABLISHES`** | **That a statutory protection from civil **and criminal** action exists for a good-faith, unrewarded responder to a **motor-vehicle accident**, covering negligence in the rendering of emergency medical **or non-medical** care, and that procedural protections on questioning and identity disclosure may be made by rules** |
| **`WHAT THE SOURCE DOES NOT ESTABLISH`** | ### **`That the protection extends beyond motor-vehicle accidents. That any organisational protection function is covered. That an organised, recurring, branded first-response activity by a body of volunteers is the same thing as a voluntary act by a bystander at a scene. That the architecture's doctrine — protect, de-escalate, call the authorities, first aid if trained, physical intervention only where reasonably necessary to protect someone from immediate harm, document and review — is protected by it. **Mandate §18: the doctrine must not be converted into legal permission, and this finding does not convert it`** |
| **`SEWAK SATHI CONSEQUENCE`** | ### **`BLOCKED PENDING PROFESSIONAL VALIDATION`, and the most important negative finding in this Part. **`ID-1`'s **`L1 … L15`** and **`RSK-4`, `RSK-5`, `RSK-8`** asked what protects a person intervening. **This research located **one** statutory protection, and its scope is confined to motor-vehicle accidents. **The gap — non-road emergency intervention, organised response, and the difference between a bystander and a deployed volunteer — is not filled by any source located, and **must not be filled by inference**. **`L4` remains, as `ID-1` recorded, the only row whose adverse answer could require the protection pillar to be redesigned or not activated** |
| **`PROFESSIONAL VALIDATION REQUIRED`** | **`YES`** |
| **`CLASSIFICATION`** | ### **`PARTIALLY SUPPORTED` — the section by consistent reproduction across five sources; **the scope question `NOT ESTABLISHED`** |

### `RF-26` — mandatory reporting of offences against children, and the institutional head's exposure

| Field | **Content** |
|---|---|
| **`RESEARCH ID`** | **`RF-26`** |
| **`ARCHITECTURAL ID`** | **`PVR-22`; **`LD-18`, `LD-23`; **`RSK-10`, `RSK-11`; **`CQ-26`; **`SR-31`; **`C-22`; **`A-09`, `A-B6`; **`L12`; **`UD-08`** |
| **`QUESTION`** | **Who must report a child sexual offence, and what is the consequence of failing to report?** |
| **`LEGAL / TAX DOMAIN`** | **Safeguarding; **criminal law; **liability** |
| **`RESEARCH FINDING`** | **The available sources indicate that **s.19(1) of the Protection of Children from Sexual Offences Act, 2012** places a duty on **any person** who has knowledge that an offence under the Act has been committed, or who apprehends that such an offence is likely to be committed, to report it to the **Special Juvenile Police Unit or the local police**; **that **s.19(2)** addresses the recording of such information; **that **s.20** places a special duty on media personnel, hotel staff and photographic studios; **and that **s.21** punishes failure. **Section 21(1)** provides that any person who fails to report the commission of an offence under s.19(1) or s.20, or who fails to record such an offence under s.19(2), **shall be punished with imprisonment of either description for a term which may extend to six months, or with fine, or with both**. **Section 21(2)** provides that **any person who is in charge of a company or institution, by whatever name called, and who fails to report the commission of such an offence in respect of a subordinate under his control, shall be punished with imprisonment for a term which may extend to one year, and with fine**. **Section 21(3)** provides that sub-section (1) **shall not apply to a child**. **The Act is gender-neutral in its protection of children, was enacted on **19 June 2012**, came into force on **14 November 2012**, was amended by the POCSO Amendment Act, **2019**, has forty-six sections, and provides for Special Courts, child-friendly reporting and evidence-recording, trial in camera without revealing the child's identity, presumptions in favour of the prosecution, and compensation for rehabilitation. **Offences are treated as aggravated where the child is mentally ill or where the abuse is committed by a person in a position of trust or authority vis-à-vis the child. **Medical sources record that the Act requires medical examination in the least distressful manner, that **doctors must not demand legal records or documentation before initiating treatment or examination**, and that failure to report may result in six months' imprisonment or a fine under s.21** |
| **`PRIMARY SOURCE`** | **`SRC-59`**, **`SRC-61`**, **`SRC-60`** — **Tier 3**, mutually consistent on the text of ss.19, 20 and 21(1) to (3). **`SRC-62`** — **National Library of Medicine / PMC, `TIER 2`**, on the medical duties and the aggravated categories. **The Act was not obtained from India Code in this research** |
| **`SOURCE DATE / EFFECTIVE DATE`** | **Enacted **19 June 2012**; **in force **14 November 2012**; **amended **2019**. **`CURRENT`** |
| **`RELEVANT CONDITION`** | ### **`The duty is on **any person**, and is therefore **independent of the existence of any organisation, entity, contract or role**. **The enhanced penalty at s.21(2) requires that the person be **in charge of a company or institution** and that the offence be committed **by a subordinate under his control** |
| **`ENTITY DEPENDENCY`** | ### **`NO — the most important feature of the finding. The duty binds individuals now, did not wait for the entity question, and does not depend on it`** |
| **`FACT DEPENDENCY`** | **`YES` — on knowledge or apprehension of an offence** |
| **`INTERPRETATION RISK`** | **`LOW` as to the duty. **`MEDIUM` as to who is *"in charge of a company or institution"* where no company or institution exists** |
| **`WHAT THE SOURCE ACTUALLY ESTABLISHES`** | **A **universal, personal, criminal** duty to report, with a stated penalty for failure, an **enhanced** penalty for an institutional head in respect of a subordinate's offence, and an exemption for a child who fails to report** |
| **`WHAT THE SOURCE DOES NOT ESTABLISH`** | **That any offence has occurred — **`DIS-23` and **`DIS-25`** are blank**. **Whether the founder or any role-holder would be *"in charge of a company or institution"* where no company or institution exists. **And it does not establish any **organisational** duty, because there is no organisation** |
| **`SEWAK SATHI CONSEQUENCE`** | ### **`CLARIFICATION REQUIRED`, and it is one of only two findings in this research that create an obligation binding on the people involved **today**, without any entity. **`ID-1`'s **`CQ-26`** asked what safeguarding duties already bind individuals, and recorded that the statement of the duties needs no entity while the organisation's registration obligations do. **This finding supplies the answer for the duties limb. **It also means **`C-22`** — the prohibition on any activity involving minors — protects individuals from an exposure that **already attaches to them personally**, quite apart from any organisational activity** |
| **`PROFESSIONAL VALIDATION REQUIRED`** | **`YES` — on the institutional-head limb** |
| **`CLASSIFICATION`** | ### **`SUPPORTED BY SECONDARY SOURCE ONLY`** |

### `RF-27` — workplace sexual harassment: the Act's reach, the ten-employee threshold, and the two committees

| Field | **Content** |
|---|---|
| **`RESEARCH ID`** | **`RF-27`** |
| **`ARCHITECTURAL ID`** | **`PVR-22`; **`LD-18`, `LD-24`; **`RSK-10`, `RSK-12`; **`UD-09`; **`CQ-26`; **`SR-02`, `SR-31`; **the HR and Verification role** |
| **`QUESTION`** | **What workplace-harassment obligations attach, to whom, and at what threshold?** |
| **`LEGAL / TAX DOMAIN`** | **Labour law; **safeguarding; **liability** |
| **`RESEARCH FINDING`** | **The available sources indicate that the Sexual Harassment of Women at Workplace (Prevention, Prohibition and Redressal) Act, 2013, with the Rules of the same year, applies to **all workplaces — government, private and non-governmental, and any organisation, institution, undertaking or establishment**. **Every workplace employing **ten or more** persons must constitute an **Internal Committee** by written order; **where an organisation has multiple administrative divisions or units at different locations, a committee is required for each such unit; **and the requirement may apply **per office or branch** rather than per legal entity, counted on the actual on-site workforce including contractual and temporary staff. **Its composition is prescribed: **a presiding officer who is a senior woman employee; **at least two employee members, preferably with legal knowledge or experience of social work or women's safety; **and **one external member** from a non-governmental organisation or association committed to the cause of women, or a person familiar with the issues; **with **at least half** the members being women, and a minimum of four members in total. **Where a workplace has fewer than ten employees, no Internal Committee is required and complaints go to a **Local Committee** constituted by the **District Officer**, with jurisdiction over the whole district; **the Local Committee also handles complaints from women working in the unorganised sector and complaints **against the employer itself**, even where an Internal Committee exists. **Its composition is a chairperson who is an eminent woman in the field of social work committed to the cause of women; **a woman working in the block, taluka, tehsil, ward or municipality in the district; **two members from non-governmental organisations or associations committed to the cause of women or familiar with the issues, one of whom must be a woman; **and an ex-officio member who is the officer dealing with social welfare or women and child development in the district. **A complaint must be filed **within three months** of the incident, or of the last incident in a series. **Only an **aggrieved woman** may complain — the Act is not gender-neutral, and it protects women employees, visitors, clients, customers, domestic workers, interns and trainees — although an organisation may adopt a gender-neutral policy, in which case the Internal Committee's statutory powers will not apply to a complaint by a man or a person of a third gender. **Employer duties include providing a safe working environment for all women employees; **drafting and disseminating an organisational policy against sexual harassment; **constituting the Internal Committee where ten or more are employed; **displaying the consequences of sexual harassment and the committee's details in a conspicuous place; **assisting the complainant if she wishes to file a complaint with the police; **forwarding a complaint to the police where the complainant wishes it or where the respondent is a third party; **treating sexual harassment as misconduct under service rules and initiating action; **and ensuring the committee submits an **annual report** to the management and to the District Officer. **The committee's name was changed from *Internal Complaints Committee* to *Internal Committee* by the Repealing and Amending Act, 2016. **No source located in this research states that the Act was repealed or subsumed by any of the four Labour Codes** |
| **`PRIMARY SOURCE`** | ### **`SRC-64` — Department of Women and Child Development, Government of NCT of Delhi, on the Act's applicability, the Internal Committee and the Local Committee. **`TIER 1`**. **Corroborated by **`SRC-63`** (Tier 2), **`SRC-65`, `SRC-66`**** |
| **`SOURCE DATE / EFFECTIVE DATE`** | **Act of 2013 with Rules of 2013 effective from the same date; **nomenclature amended by the Repealing and Amending Act, **2016**. **`CURRENT` — subject to the condition below** |
| **`RELEVANT CONDITION`** | ### **`The ten-employee threshold determines **which** committee applies, not **whether** the Act applies. **Its continued operation alongside the four Labour Codes, in force from 21 November 2025, was **not verified against a primary source in this research**, and is recorded as **`CURRENT APPLICABILITY NOT ESTABLISHED`** on that specific point** |
| **`ENTITY DEPENDENCY`** | **`PARTLY` — the Act is expressed in terms of a *workplace* and an *employer*, not a registered entity. **The Local Committee route operates precisely where no internal mechanism exists, which is the position here** |
| **`FACT DEPENDENCY`** | **`YES` — headcount, location, and whether any person is an employee** |
| **`INTERPRETATION RISK`** | **`MEDIUM`** |
| **`WHAT THE SOURCE ACTUALLY ESTABLISHES`** | **That a harassment-complaints architecture is required of every workplace; **that below ten employees the route is a district Local Committee rather than an internal body; **that the employer's duties are specific and include dissemination, display, assistance, referral, disciplinary treatment and an annual report; **and that the Local Committee exists because very small workplaces cannot credibly run their own process** |
| **`WHAT THE SOURCE DOES NOT ESTABLISH`** | **That the Act survives the Labour Codes unchanged. **That any person here is an employee. **That the founder is an *employer*. **And critically: **it does not establish that the absence of an Internal Committee is a breach — because below ten employees none is required, and the Local Committee route applies** |
| **`SEWAK SATHI CONSEQUENCE`** | **`CLARIFICATION REQUIRED`, and it supplies a route the architecture does not have. **`ID-1`'s **`CQ-26`** and the safeguarding blockers **`BLK-A-05 … BLK-A-08`** proceeded on the basis that an internal mechanism would have to be built. **This finding indicates that **a district-level Local Committee already exists by statute**, and that for a workplace below ten employees the obligation is to ensure people know how to reach it — **not to constitute an internal body**. **That is a materially less costly compliance path and it is available **without an entity**. **It is recorded as a clarification and not as a decision, and **no policy is drafted here** |
| **`PROFESSIONAL VALIDATION REQUIRED`** | **`YES` — on survival under the Codes, and on the interaction with the architecture's own protection doctrine** |
| **`CLASSIFICATION`** | ### **`SUPPORTED BY PRIMARY SOURCE`** |

### `RF-28` — social security: the thresholds at which obligations attach, and their irreversibility

| Field | **Content** |
|---|---|
| **`RESEARCH ID`** | **`RF-28`** |
| **`ARCHITECTURAL ID`** | **`PVR-22`, `PVR-03`; **`LD-09`, `LD-10`; **`TX-04`, `TX-05`; **`CD-12`; **`CQ-05`, `CQ-06`, `CQ-33`; **waterfall priority 2; **CEA `F-11`** |
| **`QUESTION`** | **At what headcount and wage levels do provident fund, employees' state insurance and related obligations attach?** |
| **`LEGAL / TAX DOMAIN`** | **Labour law; **tax; **social security** |
| **`RESEARCH FINDING`** | **The available sources indicate, for the position as administered: **Provident fund** — the Employees' Provident Funds and Miscellaneous Provisions Act, 1952 applies to every establishment employing **twenty or more** persons, with registration required within one month of crossing the threshold; **the count includes full-time, part-time, contractual and temporary workers, and workers engaged through a contractor; **an employee whose provident-fund wages are up to **₹15,000 per month** at joining must be enrolled, with participation above that ceiling voluntary or permitted under para 26(6) of the Scheme with the approval of the Assistant or Regional Provident Fund Commissioner; **contributions are **twelve per cent** by the employee and **twelve per cent** by the employer, of which **8.33 per cent** goes to the Employees' Pension Scheme computed on wages up to the ₹15,000 ceiling — a maximum of **₹1,250 per month** — with the balance of 3.67 per cent to the provident fund account, plus **0.5 per cent** for deposit-linked insurance and **0.5 per cent** administrative charges; **where an establishment with fewer than twenty employees registers voluntarily, the employer rate reduces to **ten per cent**; **members first joining on or after **1 September 2014** with wages above ₹15,000 are generally not enrolled in the pension scheme, the employer's whole twelve per cent going to the provident fund account; **and **once an establishment is covered it remains covered even if headcount later falls below twenty**. **Employees' state insurance** — applies to non-seasonal factories and notified establishments employing **ten or more** persons, and in a few states **twenty**, in a notified area, for employees with gross wages up to **₹21,000 per month**, raised to **₹25,000** for persons with disability; **contributions are **3.25 per cent** by the employer and **0.75 per cent** by the employee, a total of four per cent, and are nil for employees earning up to **₹176 per day**; **coverage has been extended under s.1(5) to shops, hotels, restaurants, road motor transport undertakings, cinemas including preview theatres, newspaper establishments, establishments engaged in insurance business, non-banking financial companies, port trusts, airport authorities and warehousing establishments** |
| **`PRIMARY SOURCE`** | **`SRC-69`** — **Tier 2**; **`SRC-67`, `SRC-68`, `SRC-70`, `SRC-71`** — **Tier 3**, mutually consistent. **No EPFO or ESIC primary document was obtained in this research** |
| **`SOURCE DATE / EFFECTIVE DATE`** | **The 1952 Act and the 1948 Act as administered; **the **1 September 2014** pension-scheme date is specific. **`CURRENT APPLICABILITY NOT ESTABLISHED AS TO THE CODE ON SOCIAL SECURITY, 2020`** — the Code is in force from 21 November 2025 with the provisions relating to employees' state insurance, maternity, gratuity and the governance bodies active, and the mechanical provident-fund provisions active while the existing Scheme continues, per **`SRC-11`**, but the thresholds under the Code's own Rules were **not located**** |
| **`RELEVANT CONDITION`** | **Establishment headcount, notified area, wage ceiling at joining, and the *once covered, always covered* rule. **Every threshold is an **establishment** test, not a legal-form test** |
| **`ENTITY DEPENDENCY`** | **`NO` — the thresholds attach to an *establishment*, whatever its form** |
| **`FACT DEPENDENCY`** | **`YES` — headcount and wages, neither of which exists** |
| **`INTERPRETATION RISK`** | **`MEDIUM` — the figures are consistent across five sources but none is primary, and the Code's Rules may alter them** |
| **`WHAT THE SOURCE ACTUALLY ESTABLISHES`** | **That the architecture's statutory-dues obligation at **waterfall priority 2** is a **threshold** obligation rather than a universal one; **that the thresholds are headcount and wage based; **and that crossing them is **irreversible** in the provident-fund case** |
| **`WHAT THE SOURCE DOES NOT ESTABLISH`** | **Any figure applicable to Sewak Sathi. **Every number above is a statement of the **general** threshold, tagged **`VERIFIED — SECONDARY`**, and **none is adopted as an architectural figure**. **`ID-1` mandate §25 and this task's mandate §14 both forbid that. **It does not establish the position under the Code on Social Security as against the 1952 and 1948 Acts** |
| **`SEWAK SATHI CONSEQUENCE`** | ### **`CLARIFICATION REQUIRED`. **Waterfall priority 2 — *"statutory dues where applicable"* — has had no researched content until now. **This finding supplies its shape: **a set of headcount and wage thresholds, a two-working-day settlement rule on separation (**`RF-10`**), a twelve-per-cent-plus-twelve-per-cent contribution structure, and an irreversible coverage rule. **The **irreversibility** is the most architecturally significant element, because headcount is therefore a **one-way gate**: **once twenty people are employed the obligation does not lapse if the number falls. **CEA **`F-11`** — that eleven people cannot supply four independent hands — sits close to a threshold that does not reverse, and must be planned against rather than discovered** |
| **`PROFESSIONAL VALIDATION REQUIRED`** | **`YES` — every figure, against the Code and its Rules** |
| **`CLASSIFICATION`** | ### **`SUPPORTED BY SECONDARY SOURCE ONLY`** |

## VII.3 What `PVR-22` now looks like

**[P]** `[PROPOSED]`. **A synthesis of `RF-18 … RF-28`. It states how far research moved the question. It does not answer it, and it says nothing about whether the four people are liable or protected.**

| Exposure | **The regime located** | **Its conditions** | **Entity dependency** | **Position after research** |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Trustee liability for breach** | **Indian Trusts Act ss.23, 24, 26, 27 — **`RF-18`, `RF-19`** | **A trust, a trustee, trust property, a beneficiary, and a breach. **Joint exposure for the whole loss; **no set-off; **passive trusteeship is itself a route to liability; **indemnity defeated by fraud** | **Trust form only** | ### **`ENTITY-DEPENDENT — PRIMARY SOURCE LOCATED`** |
| **Personal liability for corporate debts on fraud** | **Companies Act ss.339, 340, 341, 447 — **`RF-21`** | **Winding up **and** intent to defraud or a fraudulent purpose, or knowing participation. **Narrow and fault-based** | **Company form only** | ### **`ENTITY-DEPENDENT — PRIMARY SOURCE LOCATED, AND THE EXPOSURE IS NARROW`** |
| **Personal liability for unrecoverable company tax** | **Income-tax Act, 1961 s.179, on the available source carried into the 2025 Act in substantially the same terms — **`RF-22`** | **A **private company**; **tax due; **tax unrecoverable from the company; **directorship during the relevant previous year. **Reverse onus on the director** | **Private company form only** | ### **`ENTITY-DEPENDENT — SUBSTANCE LOCATED; CURRENT SECTION NUMBER IN CONFLICT`** |
| **LLP partner liability** | **LLP Act ss.27, 28, 30 — **`RF-23`** | **Not liable solely by being a partner; **liable for own wrongful act; **unlimited on fraud; **designated partners liable to all penalties, with a deeming rule where the office is vacant** | **LLP form only** | ### **`ENTITY-DEPENDENT — SECONDARY SOURCE, AND THE DEEMING RULE IS THE MATERIAL RISK`** |
| **Partnership partner liability** | **Partnership Act ss.18, 19, 25 — **`RF-24`** | **Joint and several, **unlimited**, for all acts of the firm in contract and in tort, while a partner** | **Partnership form only** | ### **`ENTITY-DEPENDENT — SECONDARY SOURCE, AND THE STRONGEST EXPOSURE LOCATED`** |
| **Society: vesting of property, and who is sued** | **Societies Registration Act ss.5, 6, 8, 10, 16 — **`RF-20`** | **Property deemed vested in the governing body; **suits in the name of a named officer or trustee; **judgment enforced against society property; **members personally suable for arrears, detention or damage** | **Society form only** | ### **`SOURCE CONFLICT — LEGAL PERSONALITY CONTESTED AT CONSTITUTION BENCH LEVEL`** |
| **Emergency assistance** | **Motor Vehicles Act s.134A — **`RF-25`** | **An accident **involving a motor vehicle**; **good faith; **voluntary; **no expectation of reward. **Covers negligence in rendering medical or non-medical care** | ### **`NO — attaches to a person`** | ### **`LOCATED, AND ITS SCOPE IS NARROW. THE NON-ROAD CASE IS NOT ESTABLISHED`** |
| **Child protection reporting** | **POCSO Act ss.19, 20, 21 — **`RF-26`** | **Knowledge or apprehension of an offence. **Any person. **Enhanced penalty for an institutional head in respect of a subordinate** | ### **`NO — binds individuals now`** | ### **`LOCATED. THE ONLY CRIMINAL DUTY FOUND THAT BINDS THE PEOPLE INVOLVED WITHOUT ANY ENTITY`** |
| **Workplace harassment** | **POSH Act, 2013 — **`RF-27`** | **A workplace and an employer. **Ten or more employees require an Internal Committee; **below that, the district Local Committee route applies** | **`PARTLY`** | ### **`LOCATED, INCLUDING A ROUTE THAT EXISTS WITHOUT ANY INTERNAL BODY`** |
| **Social security contributions** | **EPF Act, 1952; **ESI Act, 1948; **Code on Social Security, 2020 — **`RF-28`** | **Headcount and wage thresholds; **coverage irreversible once crossed** | **`NO` — establishment-based** | ### **`LOCATED AS SECONDARY; THE THRESHOLDS UNDER THE CODE ARE NOT ESTABLISHED`** |
| **Volunteer status** | **No source located — **`RF-08`** | **The statutory categories are built around **earning**. **No definition of *volunteer* was found in any source located** | **`NO`** | ### **`NOT ESTABLISHED`** |

> ### **`ELEVEN EXPOSURES RESEARCHED. TWO REGIMES BIND THE PEOPLE INVOLVED TODAY, WITHOUT ANY ENTITY: THE POCSO REPORTING DUTY, WHICH IS CRIMINAL AND UNIVERSAL, AND THE GOOD SAMARITAN PROTECTION, WHICH IS NARROW AND CONFINED TO MOTOR-VEHICLE ACCIDENTS. SIX REGIMES ARE WHOLLY ENTITY-DEPENDENT AND CANNOT BE ASSESSED UNTIL A FORM IS CHOSEN. ONE IS A SOURCE CONFLICT AT THE HIGHEST JUDICIAL LEVEL. ONE IS NOT ESTABLISHED AT ALL. AND NOTHING IN THIS TABLE SAYS THAT ANY PERSON IS, OR IS NOT, PERSONALLY LIABLE.`**

---

# PART VIII — `SR-01` / `LDR-U08`: ENTITY RESEARCH, WITHOUT SELECTING AN ENTITY

## VIII.1 The rule this Part obeys

**[P]** Mandate §5 and §11 of this task; **`ID-1`** mandate §10; **Rules `G-15 … G-20`**; **`OPT-A … OPT-J`**.

> ### **`NO ENTITY IS SELECTED HERE. NO ENTITY IS RECOMMENDED, RANKED OR PREFERRED. WHERE THE LAW DIFFERS BY ENTITY TYPE, A COMPARISON IS BUILT AND THE WORD `ENTITY-DEPENDENT` IS USED UNTIL THE STRUCTURE IS ACTUALLY SELECTED. EVERY CELL OF THE MATRIX AT §VIII.4 CARRIES ONE OF THREE LABELS — `SOURCE`, `CONDITION` OR `UNKNOWN` — AND NO GAP IS FILLED FROM GENERAL KNOWLEDGE.`**

| Label | **Meaning** | **The test** |
|---|---|---|
| ### **`SOURCE`** | **A source located in this research states the position for that form** | **A `SRC-nn` reference exists and the proposition is what the source says** |
| ### **`CONDITION`** | **A source states the position, but subject to a condition that is not satisfied or not knowable** | **The condition is stated in the cell or cross-referenced** |
| ### **`UNKNOWN`** | **No source located in this research states the position for that form** | **Not filled from general knowledge, and not inferred from a neighbouring form** |

## VIII.2 The findings

### `RF-29` — a Section 8 company: the licence, the prohibitions, and the obligations

| Field | **Content** |
|---|---|
| **`RESEARCH ID`** | **`RF-29`** |
| **`ARCHITECTURAL ID`** | **`SR-01`, `LDR-U08`; **`OPT-A`; **`FC-01 … FC-04`, `FC-E1 … FC-E8`; **`GC-01`; **`CQ-01`, `CQ-04`, `CQ-29`; **`ID-2`** |
| **`QUESTION`** | **What does Indian company law require of a company formed with charitable objects, and what does it prohibit?** |
| **`LEGAL / TAX DOMAIN`** | **Company law** |
| **`RESEARCH FINDING`** | **The available sources reproduce **s.8 of the Companies Act, 2013**, headed *"Formulation of companies with charitable objects, etc."* **Sub-section (1)** provides that where it is proved to the satisfaction of the Central Government that a person or an association of persons proposes to form a company that **(a)** has in its objects the promotion of commerce, art, science, sports, education, research, social welfare, religion, charity, protection of environment or any such other object; **(b)** intends to apply its profits, if any, or other income in promoting its objects; **and (c)** intends to prohibit the payment of any dividend to its members — **the Central Government may, by licence issued in such manner as may be prescribed and on such conditions as it deems fit, allow that person or association to be registered as a limited company under the section, without the addition to its name of the word *"Limited"* or the words *"Private Limited"*, and thereupon the Registrar shall, on application in the prescribed form, register it. **Sub-section (2)** provides that a company registered under the section **shall enjoy all the privileges and be subject to all the obligations of limited companies**. **Sub-section (3)** provides that **a firm may be a member**. **Sub-section (4)(i)** provides that such a company **shall not alter the provisions of its memorandum or articles except with the previous approval of the Central Government**. **Sub-section (5)** allows an existing company formed with those objects and subject to those restrictions to be licensed under the section. **Sub-section (6)** provides that the Central Government **may by order revoke the licence** if the company contravenes any requirement of the section or any condition of the licence, **or if the affairs of the company are conducted fraudulently, or in a manner violative of the objects of the company or prejudicial to public interest**. **Sub-section (8)** provides that where a licence is revoked and the Central Government is satisfied it is essential in the public interest, it may order **amalgamation** with another company registered under the section and having similar objects. **Sub-section (10)** provides that such a company **shall amalgamate only with another company registered under the section and having similar objects**. **Secondary sources record, in addition: **no minimum paid-up capital is required; **a minimum of two directors and two members for a private Section 8 company and three directors for a public one, with the same person capable of being both director and member; **a one person company cannot be formed as a Section 8 company; **names typically use words such as Foundation, Forum, Association, Council or Federation; **on winding up, remaining assets are not distributed to members but pass to another Section 8 company with similar objects; **and the general company obligations listed at **`RF-16`** apply, including s.12, s.92, s.128, s.137, s.139, s.149, s.166 and s.173** |
| **`PRIMARY SOURCE`** | ### **`SRC-46` — reproduces s.8(1) to (10) substantially in full. **`TIER 3 HOST, TIER 1 CONTENT`. **Corroborated by **`SRC-47`, `SRC-48`, `SRC-49`, `SRC-49a`**. **The Companies Act text was **not obtained from India Code or the Ministry of Corporate Affairs in this research**** |
| **`SOURCE DATE / EFFECTIVE DATE`** | **Companies Act, 2013, in force from 1 April 2014 in stages; **s.8 is current. **`CURRENT`. **The winding-up and asset-transfer limb at s.8(9) was **not located in text** and is marked **`VERIFIED — SECONDARY`**** |
| **`RELEVANT CONDITION`** | ### **`THREE CUMULATIVE CONDITIONS AT s.8(1), ALL OF WHICH MUST BE PROVED TO THE SATISFACTION OF THE CENTRAL GOVERNMENT: the objects, the intention to apply profits in promoting them, and the intention to prohibit dividend. **The licence is granted **on such conditions as the Central Government deems fit** — so the conditions are not fixed by the statute alone. **Alteration of the memorandum or articles requires **previous** Central Government approval. **Revocation is available for fraud, for conduct violative of the objects, or for conduct prejudicial to the public interest** |
| **`ENTITY DEPENDENCY`** | **`N/A — this finding **is** about one entity form. **It is recorded for comparison and selects nothing** |
| **`FACT DEPENDENCY`** | **`NO` as to the law; **`YES` as to whether the architecture's four pillars fall within the listed objects — **`FC-01 … FC-04`** map the pillars, and whether *"protection of environment"* and *"social welfare"* reach a first-response activity is a question for a professional** |
| **`INTERPRETATION RISK`** | **`MEDIUM` — the objects clause is broad, but whether the protection pillar and the AI-business pillar fall within it is not addressed by any source located** |
| **`WHAT THE SOURCE ACTUALLY ESTABLISHES`** | **That a company form exists in Indian law which is **licensed rather than merely registered**, whose licence is conditional, which **must prohibit dividend to members**, whose memorandum and articles cannot be altered without prior Central Government approval, which is nonetheless **subject to all the obligations of a limited company**, and which can have its licence **revoked** for conduct violative of its objects or prejudicial to the public interest** |
| **`WHAT THE SOURCE DOES NOT ESTABLISH`** | ### **`That this form is available for, suitable for, or capable of carrying the four pillars. That the prohibition on dividend resolves `GC-01` — it does not address waterfall priorities 4, 5 and 8, which are **compensation**, a **service fee** and **founder upside**, and not dividends. That a Section 8 company is a *"private company"* for **`RF-22`**. That any licence would be granted. **No candidate is preferred by this finding`** |
| **`SEWAK SATHI CONSEQUENCE`** | ### **`CLARIFICATION REQUIRED`, AND IT SHARPENS `GC-01` RATHER THAN RESOLVING IT. **`ID-1` §IX.6 recorded the conflict as: **the eight-priority waterfall against *"restrictions on profit distribution and founder upside"***. **This research locates the statutory mechanism by which that restriction operates in a Section 8 company — **a licence condition, enforceable by revocation, with prior approval required for any alteration of the memorandum** — and it also shows that the restriction is expressed as a prohibition on **dividend**, which is a narrower concept than **founder compensation at priority 4** or **founder upside at priority 8**. **Whether those priorities survive in this form is therefore **not** answered by the prohibition on dividend, and remains a professional question. **This is a genuine narrowing of **`GC-01`** — from *"is the waterfall compatible?"* to *"which of the three contested priorities is a distribution, and which is a cost?"* — and it is recorded as such, not resolved** |
| **`PROFESSIONAL VALIDATION REQUIRED`** | **`YES`** |
| **`CLASSIFICATION`** | ### **`SUPPORTED BY SECONDARY SOURCE ONLY` — the statutory text is consistently reproduced but was not obtained from a Tier 1 host** |

### `RF-30` — foreign contribution: registration, prior permission, and the three-year rule

| Field | **Content** |
|---|---|
| **`RESEARCH ID`** | **`RF-30`** |
| **`ARCHITECTURAL ID`** | **`SR-01`; **`TX-06`, `TX-15`; **`CQ-17`, `CQ-18`; **`DIS-07`, `DIS-14`; **`BLK-B-02`; **funding of the seven venture categories** |
| **`QUESTION`** | **What must an organisation do before it may accept a foreign contribution?** |
| **`LEGAL / TAX DOMAIN`** | **Tax; **regulatory; **entity law** |
| **`RESEARCH FINDING`** | **The available sources indicate that **s.11 of the Foreign Contribution (Regulation) Act, 2010** provides that unless a person having a definite cultural, economic, educational, religious or social programme obtains a **certificate of registration** or **prior permission** from the Central Government, that person shall not accept any foreign contribution. **For **registration**, the sources record that the applicant must be registered under the Societies Registration Act, 1860, the Indian Trusts Act, 1882 or s.8 of the Companies Act, 2013; **must have undertaken reasonable activities in its chosen field for the benefit of society; **must **normally have been in existence for at least three years**; **must have spent a minimum amount on its core activities, excluding administrative expenditure, over the preceding three financial years, evidenced by audited statements of accounts and activity reports; **and must have a definite programme for the utilisation of the foreign contribution. **The minimum-expenditure figure is **reported differently across sources**: **three sources state ₹15 lakh, and two state ₹10 lakh — recorded as **`SCF-03`** and not resolved. **Registration is reported as valid for **five years**, renewable, with renewal applications required before expiry. **For **prior permission**, available to an association in its formative stage, the sources record that there is **no minimum age and no minimum expenditure requirement**, but that the applicant must submit a **specific commitment letter from the donor** indicating the amount of foreign contribution, the identity of the donor, and the specific activity or project for which it is given, together with a reasonable project for the benefit of society. **Prior permission may be granted for the receipt of a **specific amount from a specific donor for specific activities or projects**, and can convert to normal registration after three years of utilisation. **All sources record that a designated **FCRA account must be opened with the State Bank of India, New Delhi Main Branch**, and one source records its IFSC. **Other conditions recorded include registration on the NITI Aayog Darpan portal, a functional registered office, possession of a 12A registration certificate, a government fee, and a processing period of ninety to one hundred and twenty working days. **One source records additional eligibility constraints: **that the applicant must not have a parent non-governmental organisation already registered under the Act, that a majority of the board must not sit on the board of another registered organisation, that the parent organisation if any must not be based abroad, and that no foreigner may be on the board** |
| **`PRIMARY SOURCE`** | **`SRC-55`** — **Tier 2**, on s.11 and the eligibility criteria. **`SRC-56`, `SRC-56a`, `SRC-57`, `SRC-57a`** — **Tier 3**. **The Foreign Contribution (Regulation) Act, 2010 and its Rules were **not obtained from a Ministry of Home Affairs source in this research**** |
| **`SOURCE DATE / EFFECTIVE DATE`** | **Act of 2010, amended in **2020**, with Rules of 2011 as amended. **`CURRENT`, but **the minimum-expenditure figure is in conflict and the amendment history was not traced to a notification** |
| **`RELEVANT CONDITION`** | ### **`THE THREE-YEAR RULE IS THE ARCHITECTURALLY DECISIVE CONDITION: normal registration requires three years of prior existence and three years of audited core-activity expenditure. **A newly constituted organisation **cannot** meet it, and must use prior permission, which requires a named donor and a named project** |
| **`ENTITY DEPENDENCY`** | **`YES` — the eligible forms are the society, the trust and the Section 8 company. **No source located extends eligibility to an LLP, a partnership or a proprietorship** |
| **`FACT DEPENDENCY`** | **`YES` — whether any foreign source of funds is contemplated at all. **`DIS-07` and **`DIS-14`** are blank** |
| **`INTERPRETATION RISK`** | **`MEDIUM` — the expenditure threshold conflicts, and the board-composition conditions are recorded by one source only** |
| **`WHAT THE SOURCE ACTUALLY ESTABLISHES`** | **That foreign contribution is **prohibited by default** and permitted only under a certificate of registration or a prior permission; **that the registration route requires three years of existence and audited core-activity expenditure; **that the prior-permission route is available immediately but is **donor-specific and project-specific**; **that a designated bank account with a specified public-sector bank in a specified city is mandatory; **and that the eligible forms are three** |
| **`WHAT THE SOURCE DOES NOT ESTABLISH`** | **The minimum-expenditure figure. **Whether the architecture's activities constitute a *"definite cultural, economic, educational, religious or social programme"*. **Whether the board-composition conditions are current. **And it does not establish that any foreign contribution is contemplated here** |
| **`SEWAK SATHI CONSEQUENCE`** | ### **`CLARIFICATION REQUIRED`, AND IT IS A SEQUENCING CONSTRAINT THE ARCHITECTURE DID NOT HAVE. **`ID-1` **`DIS-07`** asks whether any foreign source of funds is contemplated and **`TX-06`** asks about donations. **This research establishes that if the answer is yes, **a newly constituted entity cannot obtain normal registration for three years**, and can receive only a specific amount from a specific donor for a specific project. **Any funding plan that assumes foreign contribution at launch is therefore **not merely unvalidated but structurally unavailable on the located source**. **This bears directly on CEA **`F-10`** — the unfunded mandate — and on the funding map at CEA §XII.2** |
| **`PROFESSIONAL VALIDATION REQUIRED`** | **`YES` — the expenditure threshold, the board conditions and the current Rules** |
| **`CLASSIFICATION`** | ### **`PARTIALLY SUPPORTED` — the three-year rule and the two routes by Tier 2; **the expenditure threshold `SOURCE CONFLICT`** |

| **`SEWAK SATHI CONSEQUENCE`** | **`CLARIFICATION REQUIRED`. **`ID-1`'s **`CQ-01 … CQ-04`** asked for the minimum constitutive document set per candidate. **This research supplies the **minimum number of persons** for five candidates and **nothing** for five others, and supplies the constitutive act for four. **It also establishes a constraint the architecture has not previously recorded: **a society requires **seven** members, which is a headcount requirement, not merely a document requirement, and **CEA `F-11`** records that the available people cannot supply four independent hands** |

|---|---|---|---|
| **`OPT-A` — Section 8 company** | **A **licence** from the Central Government, issued in the prescribed manner and on such conditions as it deems fit, followed by registration by the Registrar on application in the prescribed form; **a memorandum and articles that cannot thereafter be altered without **previous** Central Government approval** | **Two directors and two members for a private Section 8 company; **three directors for a public one; **a firm may be a member; **a one person company is excluded** | ### **`SOURCE`** |
| **`OPT-B` — registered society** | **Registration with the Registrar of Societies of the concerned state, on a memorandum of association and rules and regulations** | ### **`Seven members at least`** | ### **`SOURCE`** — **but legal personality is **`SCF-01`**** |
| **`OPT-C` — public charitable trust** | **The Indian Trusts Act, 1882 was located from India Code and governs **private** trusts; **public charitable trusts in several states are governed by state public-trust legislation, which was **not researched**. **No constitutive act or minimum number was located** | **Not located** | ### **`UNKNOWN` — state legislation not researched** |
| **`OPT-D` — ordinary private company** | **Incorporation under the Companies Act, 2013; **a registered office within thirty days verified in Form INC-22; **first auditors appointed by the board within thirty days** | **Two persons minimum for a private company, three for a public one; **two directors minimum for a private company** | ### **`SOURCE`** |
| **`OPT-E` — limited liability partnership** | **Incorporation under the LLP Act, 2008; **an LLP agreement** | **At least two individuals as designated partners, at least one resident in India by reference to a stay of not less than 182 days in the preceding year; **no maximum number of partners; **a body corporate may be a partner and may nominate an individual** | ### **`SOURCE`** |
| **`OPT-F` — partnership** | **An agreement between persons to share the profits of a business carried on by all or any of them acting for all; **registration is available and a registration certificate is required for a bank account under **`RF-33`**** | **Not located** | ### **`CONDITION`** |
| **`OPT-G` — proprietorship** | **Not located in any source** | **Not located** | ### **`UNKNOWN`** |
| **`OPT-H` — hybrid charitable plus commercial** | **Not located as a single regime. **It would be a combination of two or more of the above, and **`RF-30`** indicates that the charitable limb's eligibility for foreign contribution is limited to three forms** | **The sum of its parts** | ### **`CONDITION`** |
| **`OPT-I` — parent or holding plus operating entity** | **Not located. **`ID-1` records **`UE-24`**: **removal of the block requires at least two legal persons, of which at most one can be constituted at a time** | **At least two legal persons** | ### **`UNKNOWN` — and blocked by **`UE-24`**** |
| **`OPT-J` — other, counsel-identified** | **By definition not researched** | **Not applicable** | ### **`UNKNOWN`** |
